I’m tired all the time, but then when I actually get into bed, my mind just… won’t shut off.\n\nI’ll be physically exhausted, like my body feels heavy and I’m desperate to sleep, but I’ll lie there replaying things from school — assessment deadlines, whether I’ve marked enough, whether I missed something important, some tiny mistake I made in a classroom conversation that probably no one else even noticed. It just loops. I’ll think, okay, stop thinking about it, you need to sleep, and then I’m still lying there half an hour later going over the same thing again.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up really early, around 4 a.m. a lot of mornings. Not just briefly waking up and rolling over — I mean properly awake, with that awful feeling like the day has already started in my head. I try to get back to sleep, but usually I just sort of lie there, checking the time, calculating how little sleep I’ve had, and then worrying about how I’m going to manage the day. So yes, sleep has been really difficult lately.\n\nDoctor: If school were out of session and you had no work responsibilities for a week, do you think you would still feel this loss of interest and emotional heaviness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, school definitely makes it worse, because there’s always something to worry about or prepare for, but it doesn’t feel like this is only work.\n\nIf I had a week off, I don’t think I’d suddenly be excited about things again. Softball would still feel like something I should go to rather than something I want to do. Book club would probably still feel like another social obligation, even if I had technically “rested.” And my garden… I know that sounds small, but that used to be something I’d naturally drift toward when I had free time. Now I look at it and it’s like I can see what needs doing, but I don’t feel that little pull to do it.\n\nEven robotics club — which is work-adjacent, I suppose, but it used to feel different from the rest of school — it used to give me energy. Now I mostly just go through the motions, or I cancel things when I can, because none of it really feels enjoyable anymore. So yes, I think even without school for a week, that heaviness and lack of interest would still be there.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these symptoms started to interfere noticeably with your ability to do your job or manage things at home?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m falling behind on grading and lesson planning in ways that affect my classroom, and the grading mistake scared me because I used to be meticulous. I’ve also stopped showing up for friends and family the way I used to, so most of my life has narrowed to barely getting through school and then going home exhausted.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I think that’s the part that’s started to really frighten me, honestly, because it’s not just that I feel bad privately and then I can still function normally. It’s showing up in very concrete ways.

At school, I’m falling behind on grading in a way that I don’t usually. I’ve always been the kind of teacher who stays on top of marks and feedback — maybe not perfectly, but reliably. Lately I keep putting it off, or I sit down to do it and I just stare at the stack or the online submissions and it feels impossible to start. Then it piles up, and the students are waiting longer for feedback, and I know that affects the classroom because I’m not as prepared for where they actually are.

Lesson planning has been the same. I’m still getting through the day, but it feels much more like scrambling. I used to have a sense of where a unit was going, what I wanted them to get out of it, what needed adjusting. Now I’m doing more last-minute planning than I’m comfortable admitting. I can get through a lesson, but it doesn’t feel like I’m teaching the way I normally would.

There was also a grading mistake recently that really scared me. Not because it was catastrophic, exactly, but because I used to be so meticulous about that kind of thing. I’d double-check, I’d notice discrepancies, I’d catch myself. And this time I missed something I really should have caught, and when I realized it, I just felt this awful sinking feeling, like… this isn’t me, or at least it didn’t used to be.

At home, I’m managing the absolute basics, but not much beyond that. And socially, I’ve really stopped showing up for people the way I used to. I don’t call family back as quickly, I avoid making plans with friends, I cancel things or let messages sit because even replying feels like another task. So my life has gotten very small. It’s basically get through school somehow, come home exhausted, do the minimum, and then start over.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I’ve started having thoughts that really frightened me. Not like I have some plan, or that I want to do anything, but I keep catching myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. And then sometimes, especially on the drive home after work, my mind goes to this awful place where I wonder if my family, or even my students, would somehow be better off without me.\n\nI know that sounds terrible saying it out loud. I’m a teacher, I’m supposed to be responsible and steady, and I don’t want to act on those thoughts. That’s part of why I came in, because the fact that they’re showing up at all really scares me.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts come up, do they feel like unwanted thoughts that pop into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s exactly what scares me. It’s more like I’ll be driving home from work, and out of nowhere this thought comes in — like maybe it would be easier if I didn’t wake up, or maybe my family and my students would be better off without me — and then I feel horrified that I even thought it.\n\nI don’t want to act on it. I really don’t. But the fact that it pops into my mind at all makes me feel like something is very wrong, and then I get scared of myself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts show up, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to make sure you wouldn’t act on them?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so? Not in a repeated way, at least. But I don’t think I have a ritual or a specific thing I do over and over to prove I won’t act on them. And the robotics club at school — that used to be one of the parts of teaching that made me feel really energized, seeing the students get excited and problem-solve.\n\nLately, though, it’s like I’m physically present sometimes, but not really in it. I go through the motions. I show up because I said I would, or because people expect me to, but I’m not getting the same feeling from it. And sometimes I just cancel. I’ll tell myself I’m too tired or that I have grading to do, but if I’m honest, it’s more that none of it sounds enjoyable anymore. Even the things that used to feel like “me” just feel like obligations now.\n\nDoctor: For the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly still not sure how to say that exactly. Nearly every day sounds so definite, and I keep second-guessing myself because I don’t know if what I’ve been feeling “counts” as depressed or hopeless in the way you mean.\n\nI can say I’ve felt not okay a lot of the time these past couple weeks — kind of low, heavy, like I’m dragging myself through things. But I don’t always wake up and think, “I’m depressed.” Sometimes I just think I’m tired, or stressed, or that I need to get through the day and stop overthinking it.\n\nHopeless… I don’t know. That word feels intense. I don’t know if I’d say hopeless nearly every day. There have been moments where everything feels pointless or like I can’t see myself getting back to normal, but then I’ll also tell myself I’m being dramatic or that it’ll pass.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not completely sure. I’ve felt down a lot lately, probably more days than not, but I don’t know if I can confidently say nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, there was a specific work thing, though it didn’t feel like a single dramatic event at the time. It was more like a major change in what I was carrying.\n\nIt started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my regular teaching load. That was about… a year and seven months ago now, I think. I remember thinking I could handle it because it was important, and I cared about doing it well, and I didn’t want to be the person who said no. But it became this constant extra layer on everything I was already doing.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just a busy term, or that once we got through the next deadline it would settle. But it never really did. It’s hard to separate what started when, because teaching is always busy, but when I look back, that’s when things started changing for me. That was the point where I stopped feeling like I could quite catch up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way continuously for the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It feels like a long time, honestly, long enough that it’s hard to remember what I felt like before, but when I actually count it out, it’s been about a year and seven months.\n\nIt started around when I took over that STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my regular teaching. That’s the point I keep coming back to when I try to trace it. I don’t think I was feeling like this continuously before then, at least not in the same way. Since then, though, it’s felt pretty ongoing.\n\nDoctor: Have there been separate periods in your life before this past year and a half when you felt depressed and lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. But I don’t know that I’d describe them as the same as what’s been happening this past year and a half. I don’t remember a distinct episode where I stopped enjoying things for at least two weeks in a way that felt obvious at the time.\n\nIt’s possible I’m forgetting or minimizing it, because I tend to push through and not label things until much later. But if you’re asking whether there was a clear earlier period like this, I’d say… not that I can really identify.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. I mean, I do worry, but I don’t know where the line is between normal worrying and excessive worrying, especially when things feel like they actually need worrying about.\n\nA lot of it tends to be… practical, I suppose? Like whether I’ve missed something, whether I’ve let something slip, whether I’m keeping up properly. I’ve thought about that a lot, actually, because part of me wants to say yes, of course, if the workload disappeared then I’d feel better. It would be such a relief not to have that constant pressure sitting there.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s that simple. I can’t quite tell anymore what’s caused by the work burden and what’s just… become the way I feel. Even the robotics club at school used to be something I genuinely enjoyed — I’d get excited about the students’ ideas and the little breakthroughs they’d have. Now I still sometimes show up, or I tell myself I should show up, but it feels like I’m just sort of… performing the actions of a person who enjoys those things.\n\nA lot of the time I cancel, honestly. I’ll think about book club and instead of feeling pleased to see people or talk about the book, it just feels like another thing I have to get through. With softball, I used to like the routine of it, being outside, being with the team, moving around. Now I’ll make excuses not to go, or if I do go, I’m just waiting for it to be over.\n\nAnd the garden is maybe the strangest one, because that used to be very calming for me. I used to notice little things — new growth, weeds, what needed pruning — and now I look at it and feel almost nothing. So yes, I’d say I’ve lost interest and pleasure in most of the things I used to enjoy, and it’s been happening pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It just loops. I’ll think, okay, stop thinking about it, you need to sleep, and then I’m still lying there half an hour later going over the same thing again.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up really early, around 4 a.m. a lot of mornings. Not just briefly waking up and rolling over — I mean properly awake, with that awful feeling like the day has already started in my head. I try to get back to sleep, but usually I just sort of lie there, checking the time, calculating how little sleep I’ve had, and then worrying about how I’m going to manage the day. So yes, sleep has been really difficult lately.\n\nDoctor: If school were out of session and you had no work responsibilities for a week, do you think you would still feel this loss of interest and emotional heaviness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. But I don’t always wake up and think, “I’m depressed.” Sometimes I just think I’m tired, or stressed, or that I need to get through the day and stop overthinking it.\n\nHopeless… I don’t know. That word feels intense. I don’t know if I’d say hopeless nearly every day. There have been moments where everything feels pointless or like I can’t see myself getting back to normal, but then I’ll also tell myself I’m being dramatic or that it’ll pass.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not completely sure. I’ve felt down a lot lately, probably more days than not, but I don’t know if I can confidently say nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, there was a specific work thing, though it didn’t feel like a single dramatic event at the time. It was more like a major change in what I was carrying.\n\nIt started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my regular teaching load. That was about… a year and seven months ago now, I think. I remember thinking I could handle it because it was important, and I cared about doing it well, and I didn’t want to be the person who said no. But it became this constant extra layer on everything I was already doing.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just a busy term, or that once we got through the next deadline it would settle. But it never really did. It’s hard to separate what started when, because teaching is always busy, but when I look back, that’s when things started changing for me. That was the point where I stopped feeling like I could quite catch up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way continuously for the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It feels like a long time, honestly, long enough that it’s hard to remember what I felt like before, but when I actually count it out, it’s been about a year and seven months.\n\nIt started around when I took over that STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my regular teaching. That’s the point I keep coming back to when I try to trace it. I don’t think I was feeling like this continuously before then, at least not in the same way. Since then, though, it’s felt pretty ongoing.\n\nDoctor: Have there been separate periods in your life before this past year and a half when you felt depressed and lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I don’t think there was a clear separate period like that before this, not one that stands out to me as “yes, that was definitely two weeks where I was depressed and lost interest in everything.”\n\nI’ve had stressful patches before, definitely — I mean, I think most people have — and times where I felt low or overwhelmed, especially with work or life stuff. But I don’t know that I’d describe them as the same as what’s been happening this past year and a half. I don’t remember a distinct episode where I stopped enjoying things for at least two weeks in a way that felt obvious at the time.\n\nIt’s possible I’m forgetting or minimizing it, because I tend to push through and not label things until much later. But if you’re asking whether there was a clear earlier period like this, I’d say… not that I can really identify.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. I mean, I do worry, but I don’t know where the line is between normal worrying and excessive worrying, especially when things feel like they actually need worrying about.\n\nA lot of it tends to be… practical, I suppose? Like whether I’ve missed something, whether I’ve let something slip, whether I’m keeping up properly. But I don’t know that I sit there worrying about every area of my life all the time, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like certain things get stuck in my head and I go over them more than I probably need to.\n\nSo… I guess yes, I worry, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as excessive worry about lots of different things. I’d maybe need you to clarify what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: If that work burden were removed, do you think you’d still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve thought about that a lot, actually, because part of me wants to say yes, of course, if the workload disappeared then I’d feel better. It would be such a relief not to have that constant pressure sitting there.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s that simple. I can’t quite tell anymore what’s caused by the work burden and what’s just… become the way I feel. If it were removed, maybe I’d improve a lot, or maybe I’d still feel flat and worn down because I’ve been in that state for so long. I haven’t had a real chance to test it, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I suppose my honest answer is: I’d expect some relief, but I’m not confident I’d suddenly feel like myself again.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts come up, do they feel senseless or out of character for you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel senseless, exactly. That’s part of what scares me, I think. In the moment they can feel… almost logical, like my brain is making an argument that everyone would be less burdened if I just wasn’t there. Especially on the drive home, when I’m already exhausted and going over everything I’ve done wrong or might have failed at, I’ll catch myself wondering whether my family or even my students would be better off without me.\n\nBut then another part of me is horrified by it, because it doesn’t feel like who I want to be. I don’t want to act on those thoughts. I’m here because I’ve started having thoughts like it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up, and that really frightened me. It feels out of character in the sense that I don’t want to die, not really — I just sometimes want everything to stop, and then I feel ashamed and scared that my mind even goes there.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you lost interest or pleasure in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Nearly every day, really.\n\nIt’s not that I never do anything anymore, exactly, but the feeling has gone out of it. I used to really look forward to things like softball, and book club, and working in my garden. Even the robotics club at school used to be something I genuinely enjoyed — I’d get excited about the students’ ideas and the little breakthroughs they’d have. Now I still sometimes show up, or I tell myself I should show up, but it feels like I’m just sort of… performing the actions of a person who enjoys those things.\n\nA lot of the time I cancel, honestly. I’ll think about book club and instead of feeling pleased to see people or talk about the book, it just feels like another thing I have to get through. With softball, I used to like the routine of it, being outside, being with the team, moving around. Now I’ll make excuses not to go, or if I do go, I’m just waiting for it to be over.\n\nAnd the garden is maybe the strangest one, because that used to be very calming for me. I used to notice little things — new growth, weeds, what needed pruning — and now I look at it and feel almost nothing. So yes, I’d say I’ve lost interest and pleasure in most of the things I used to enjoy, and it’s been happening pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’m tired all the time, but then when I actually get into bed, my mind just… won’t shut off.\n\nI’ll be physically exhausted, like my body feels heavy and I’m desperate to sleep, but I’ll lie there replaying things from school — assessment deadlines, whether I’ve marked enough, whether I missed something important, some tiny mistake I made in a classroom conversation that probably no one else even noticed. It just loops. I’ll think, okay, stop thinking about it, you need to sleep, and then I’m still lying there half an hour later going over the same thing again.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up really early, around 4 a.m. a lot of mornings. Not just briefly waking up and rolling over — I mean properly awake, with that awful feeling like the day has already started in my head. I try to get back to sleep, but usually I just sort of lie there, checking the time, calculating how little sleep I’ve had, and then worrying about how I’m going to manage the day. So yes, sleep has been really difficult lately.\n\nDoctor: If school were out of session and you had no work responsibilities for a week, do you think you would still feel this loss of interest and emotional heaviness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, school definitely makes it worse, because there’s always something to worry about or prepare for, but it doesn’t feel like this is only work.\n\nIf I had a week off, I don’t think I’d suddenly be excited about things again. Softball would still feel like something I should go to rather than something I want to do. Book club would probably still feel like another social obligation, even if I had technically “rested.” And my garden… I know that sounds small, but that used to be something I’d naturally drift toward when I had free time. Now I look at it and it’s like I can see what needs doing, but I don’t feel that little pull to do it.\n\nEven robotics club — which is work-adjacent, I suppose, but it used to feel different from the rest of school — it used to give me energy. Now I mostly just go through the motions, or I cancel things when I can, because none of it really feels enjoyable anymore. So yes, I think even without school for a week, that heaviness and lack of interest would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms started to interfere noticeably with your ability to do your job or manage things at home?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that’s the part that’s started to really frighten me, honestly, because it’s not just that I feel bad privately and then I can still function normally. It’s showing up in very concrete ways.\n\nAt school, I’m falling behind on grading in a way that I don’t usually. I’ve always been the kind of teacher who stays on top of marks and feedback — maybe not perfectly, but reliably. Lately I keep putting it off, or I sit down to do it and I just stare at the stack or the online submissions and it feels impossible to start. Then it piles up, and the students are waiting longer for feedback, and I know that affects the classroom because I’m not as prepared for where they actually are.\n\nLesson planning has been the same. I’m still getting through the day, but it feels much more like scrambling. I used to have a sense of where a unit was going, what I wanted them to get out of it, what needed adjusting. Now I’m doing more last-minute planning than I’m comfortable admitting. I can get through a lesson, but it doesn’t feel like I’m teaching the way I normally would.\n\nThere was also a grading mistake recently that really scared me. Not because it was catastrophic, exactly, but because I used to be so meticulous about that kind of thing. I’d double-check, I’d notice discrepancies, I’d catch myself. And this time I missed something I really should have caught, and when I realized it, I just felt this awful sinking feeling, like… this isn’t me, or at least it didn’t used to be.\n\nAt home, I’m managing the absolute basics, but not much beyond that. And socially, I’ve really stopped showing up for people the way I used to. I don’t call family back as quickly, I avoid making plans with friends, I cancel things or let messages sit because even replying feels like another task. So my life has gotten very small. It’s basically get through school somehow, come home exhausted, do the minimum, and then start over.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yes. Definitely. It hasn’t really been just a short patch, unfortunately.

I’d say this has been going on for about… a year and seven months now, roughly. It started around the time I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. At first I thought it was just stress from that, like, okay, I’ve taken on too much and I’m overwhelmed, and once I get through the next phase it’ll lift. But it didn’t really lift.

Not like I have some plan, or that I want to do anything, but I keep catching myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. And then sometimes, especially on the drive home after work, my mind goes to this awful place where I wonder if my family, or even my students, would somehow be better off without me.\n\nI know that sounds terrible saying it out loud. I’m a teacher, I’m supposed to be responsible and steady, and I don’t want to act on those thoughts. That’s part of why I came in, because the fact that they’re showing up at all really scares me.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts come up, do they feel like unwanted thoughts that pop into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s exactly what scares me. They feel unwanted. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think that way, or like I’m making some kind of plan. It’s more like I’ll be driving home from work, and out of nowhere this thought comes in — like maybe it would be easier if I didn’t wake up, or maybe my family and my students would be better off without me — and then I feel horrified that I even thought it.\n\nI don’t want to act on it. I really don’t. But the fact that it pops into my mind at all makes me feel like something is very wrong, and then I get scared of myself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts show up, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to make sure you wouldn’t act on them?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so? Not in a repeated way, at least. I’m trying to think if there’s anything I do like that, because I don’t want to miss something important.\n\nWhen the thoughts happen, I usually just get really upset and sort of try to push them away, or I’ll sit there feeling guilty and shaken by it. But I don’t think I have a ritual or a specific thing I do over and over to prove I won’t act on them. It’s more like I feel scared that the thought happened at all, and then I keep turning it over in my head afterward, wondering what it says about me.\n\nI mean, I do sometimes reassure myself that I don’t want to do anything, or remind myself of my family and my students, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like a repeated checking thing exactly. It feels more like panic and shame after the thought shows up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days lately?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I know I haven’t been feeling like myself, obviously, or I wouldn’t be here. But “down or depressed most days” — I don’t know if I’ve been calling it that in my own head.\n\nSome days I just feel heavy, I guess, or kind of worn out emotionally. But then I’ll tell myself that everyone gets overwhelmed, especially with work and everything, so I don’t always know where the line is. I’ve been trying not to make too big a deal out of it, and then something happens like these thoughts showing up, and it makes me wonder if I’ve been brushing things off more than I should.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m sorry, that’s not a very straightforward answer. I can say I haven’t felt okay lately, but I’m not sure I know exactly how to label it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Actually, yes, probably more than I’ve wanted to admit.\n\nI used to have things I really looked forward to, not just big things, but normal parts of my week. Like softball used to be this thing where even if I was tired after school, once I got there and started warming up with everyone, I’d feel lighter. And book club too — I used to actually read the book and have opinions and enjoy the conversation. I have a little garden at home, and normally I’d be out there checking on things, fussing over the plants more than necessary. And the robotics club at school — that used to be one of the parts of teaching that made me feel really energized, seeing the students get excited and problem-solve.\n\nLately, though, it’s like I’m physically present sometimes, but not really in it. I go through the motions. I show up because I said I would, or because people expect me to, but I’m not getting the same feeling from it. And sometimes I just cancel. I’ll tell myself I’m too tired or that I have grading to do, but if I’m honest, it’s more that none of it sounds enjoyable anymore. Even the things that used to feel like “me” just feel like obligations now.\n\nDoctor: For the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly still not sure how to say that exactly. Nearly every day sounds so definite, and I keep second-guessing myself because I don’t know if what I’ve been feeling “counts” as depressed or hopeless in the way you mean.\n\nI can say I’ve felt not okay a lot of the time these past couple weeks — kind of low, heavy, like I’m dragging myself through things. But I don’t always wake up and think, “I’m depressed.” Sometimes I just think I’m tired, or stressed, or that I need to get through the day and stop overthinking it.\n\nHopeless… I don’t know. That word feels intense. I don’t know if I’d say hopeless nearly every day. There have been moments where everything feels pointless or like I can’t see myself getting back to normal, but then I’ll also tell myself I’m being dramatic or that it’ll pass.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not completely sure. I’ve felt down a lot lately, probably more days than not, but I don’t know if I can confidently say nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, there was a specific work thing, though it didn’t feel like a single dramatic event at the time. It was more like a major change in what I was carrying.\n\nIt started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my regular teaching load. That was about… a year and seven months ago now, I think. I remember thinking I could handle it because it was important, and I cared about doing it well, and I didn’t want to be the person who said no. But it became this constant extra layer on everything I was already doing.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just a busy term, or that once we got through the next deadline it would settle. But it never really did. It’s hard to separate what started when, because teaching is always busy, but when I look back, that’s when things started changing for me. That was the point where I stopped feeling like I could quite catch up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way continuously for the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It feels like a long time, honestly, long enough that it’s hard to remember what I felt like before, but when I actually count it out, it’s been about a year and seven months.\n\nIt started around when I took over that STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my regular teaching. That’s the point I keep coming back to when I try to trace it. I don’t think I was feeling like this continuously before then, at least not in the same way. Since then, though, it’s felt pretty ongoing.\n\nDoctor: Have there been separate periods in your life before this past year and a half when you felt depressed and lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I don’t think there was a clear separate period like that before this, not one that stands out to me as “yes, that was definitely two weeks where I was depressed and lost interest in everything.”\n\nI’ve had stressful patches before, definitely — I mean, I think most people have — and times where I felt low or overwhelmed, especially with work or life stuff. But I don’t know that I’d describe them as the same as what’s been happening this past year and a half. I don’t remember a distinct episode where I stopped enjoying things for at least two weeks in a way that felt obvious at the time.\n\nIt’s possible I’m forgetting or minimizing it, because I tend to push through and not label things until much later. But if you’re asking whether there was a clear earlier period like this, I’d say… not that I can really identify.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. I mean, I do worry, but I don’t know where the line is between normal worrying and excessive worrying, especially when things feel like they actually need worrying about.\n\nA lot of it tends to be… practical, I suppose? Like whether I’ve missed something, whether I’ve let something slip, whether I’m keeping up properly. But I don’t know that I sit there worrying about every area of my life all the time, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like certain things get stuck in my head and I go over them more than I probably need to.\n\nSo… I guess yes, I worry, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as excessive worry about lots of different things. I’d maybe need you to clarify what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: If that work burden were removed, do you think you’d still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve thought about that a lot, actually, because part of me wants to say yes, of course, if the workload disappeared then I’d feel better. It would be such a relief not to have that constant pressure sitting there.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s that simple. I can’t quite tell anymore what’s caused by the work burden and what’s just… become the way I feel. If it were removed, maybe I’d improve a lot, or maybe I’d still feel flat and worn down because I’ve been in that state for so long. I haven’t had a real chance to test it, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I suppose my honest answer is: I’d expect some relief, but I’m not confident I’d suddenly feel like myself again.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts come up, do they feel senseless or out of character for you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel senseless, exactly. That’s part of what scares me, I think. In the moment they can feel… almost logical, like my brain is making an argument that everyone would be less burdened if I just wasn’t there. Especially on the drive home, when I’m already exhausted and going over everything I’ve done wrong or might have failed at, I’ll catch myself wondering whether my family or even my students would be better off without me.\n\nBut then another part of me is horrified by it, because it doesn’t feel like who I want to be. I don’t want to act on those thoughts. I’m here because I’ve started having thoughts like it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up, and that really frightened me. It feels out of character in the sense that I don’t want to die, not really — I just sometimes want everything to stop, and then I feel ashamed and scared that my mind even goes there.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you lost interest or pleasure in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Nearly every day, really.\n\nIt’s not that I never do anything anymore, exactly, but the feeling has gone out of it. I used to really look forward to things like softball, and book club, and working in my garden. Even the robotics club at school used to be something I genuinely enjoyed — I’d get excited about the students’ ideas and the little breakthroughs they’d have. Now I still sometimes show up, or I tell myself I should show up, but it feels like I’m just sort of… performing the actions of a person who enjoys those things.\n\nA lot of the time I cancel, honestly. I’ll think about book club and instead of feeling pleased to see people or talk about the book, it just feels like another thing I have to get through. With softball, I used to like the routine of it, being outside, being with the team, moving around. Now I’ll make excuses not to go, or if I do go, I’m just waiting for it to be over.\n\nAnd the garden is maybe the strangest one, because that used to be very calming for me. I used to notice little things — new growth, weeds, what needed pruning — and now I look at it and feel almost nothing. So yes, I’d say I’ve lost interest and pleasure in most of the things I used to enjoy, and it’s been happening pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’m tired all the time, but then when I actually get into bed, my mind just… won’t shut off.\n\nI’ll be physically exhausted, like my body feels heavy and I’m desperate to sleep, but I’ll lie there replaying things from school — assessment deadlines, whether I’ve marked enough, whether I missed something important, some tiny mistake I made in a classroom conversation that probably no one else even noticed. It just loops. I’ll think, okay, stop thinking about it, you need to sleep, and then I’m still lying there half an hour later going over the same thing again.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up really early, around 4 a.m. a lot of mornings. Not just briefly waking up and rolling over — I mean properly awake, with that awful feeling like the day has already started in my head. I try to get back to sleep, but usually I just sort of lie there, checking the time, calculating how little sleep I’ve had, and then worrying about how I’m going to manage the day. So yes, sleep has been really difficult lately.\n\nDoctor: If school were out of session and you had no work responsibilities for a week, do you think you would still feel this loss of interest and emotional heaviness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, school definitely makes it worse, because there’s always something to worry about or prepare for, but it doesn’t feel like this is only work.\n\nIf I had a week off, I don’t think I’d suddenly be excited about things again. Softball would still feel like something I should go to rather than something I want to do. Book club would probably still feel like another social obligation, even if I had technically “rested.” And my garden… I know that sounds small, but that used to be something I’d naturally drift toward when I had free time. Now I look at it and it’s like I can see what needs doing, but I don’t feel that little pull to do it.\n\nEven robotics club — which is work-adjacent, I suppose, but it used to feel different from the rest of school — it used to give me energy. Now I mostly just go through the motions, or I cancel things when I can, because none of it really feels enjoyable anymore. So yes, I think even without school for a week, that heaviness and lack of interest would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms started to interfere noticeably with your ability to do your job or manage things at home?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that’s the part that’s started to really frighten me, honestly, because it’s not just that I feel bad privately and then I can still function normally. It’s showing up in very concrete ways.\n\nAt school, I’m falling behind on grading in a way that I don’t usually. I’ve always been the kind of teacher who stays on top of marks and feedback — maybe not perfectly, but reliably. Lately I keep putting it off, or I sit down to do it and I just stare at the stack or the online submissions and it feels impossible to start. Then it piles up, and the students are waiting longer for feedback, and I know that affects the classroom because I’m not as prepared for where they actually are.\n\nLesson planning has been the same. I’m still getting through the day, but it feels much more like scrambling. I used to have a sense of where a unit was going, what I wanted them to get out of it, what needed adjusting. Now I’m doing more last-minute planning than I’m comfortable admitting. I can get through a lesson, but it doesn’t feel like I’m teaching the way I normally would.\n\nThere was also a grading mistake recently that really scared me. Not because it was catastrophic, exactly, but because I used to be so meticulous about that kind of thing. I’d double-check, I’d notice discrepancies, I’d catch myself. And this time I missed something I really should have caught, and when I realized it, I just felt this awful sinking feeling, like… this isn’t me, or at least it didn’t used to be.\n\nAt home, I’m managing the absolute basics, but not much beyond that. And socially, I’ve really stopped showing up for people the way I used to. I don’t call family back as quickly, I avoid making plans with friends, I cancel things or let messages sit because even replying feels like another task. So my life has gotten very small. It’s basically get through school somehow, come home exhausted, do the minimum, and then start over.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and a half, was there ever a stretch of at least two weeks when you felt depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. But it didn’t really lift.\n\nAnd within that whole period, yes, there have absolutely been stretches of more than two weeks where I felt depressed nearly every day. Honestly, it’s been more continuous than I want to admit. Now I’ll make excuses not to go, or if I do go, I’m just waiting for it to be over.\n\nAnd the garden is maybe the strangest one, because that used to be very calming for me. I used to notice little things — new growth, weeds, what needed pruning — and now I look at it and feel almost nothing. So yes, I’d say I’ve lost interest and pleasure in most of the things I used to enjoy, and it’s been happening pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’m tired all the time, but then when I actually get into bed, my mind just… won’t shut off.\n\nI’ll be physically exhausted, like my body feels heavy and I’m desperate to sleep, but I’ll lie there replaying things from school — assessment deadlines, whether I’ve marked enough, whether I missed something important, some tiny mistake I made in a classroom conversation that probably no one else even noticed. It just loops. I’ll think, okay, stop thinking about it, you need to sleep, and then I’m still lying there half an hour later going over the same thing again.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up really early, around 4 a.m. a lot of mornings. Not just briefly waking up and rolling over — I mean properly awake, with that awful feeling like the day has already started in my head. I try to get back to sleep, but usually I just sort of lie there, checking the time, calculating how little sleep I’ve had, and then worrying about how I’m going to manage the day. So yes, sleep has been really difficult lately.\n\nDoctor: If school were out of session and you had no work responsibilities for a week, do you think you would still feel this loss of interest and emotional heaviness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, school definitely makes it worse, because there’s always something to worry about or prepare for, but it doesn’t feel like this is only work.\n\nIf I had a week off, I don’t think I’d suddenly be excited about things again. Softball would still feel like something I should go to rather than something I want to do. Book club would probably still feel like another social obligation, even if I had technically “rested.” And my garden… I know that sounds small, but that used to be something I’d naturally drift toward when I had free time. Now I look at it and it’s like I can see what needs doing, but I don’t feel that little pull to do it.\n\nEven robotics club — which is work-adjacent, I suppose, but it used to feel different from the rest of school — it used to give me energy. Now I mostly just go through the motions, or I cancel things when I can, because none of it really feels enjoyable anymore. So yes, I think even without school for a week, that heaviness and lack of interest would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms started to interfere noticeably with your ability to do your job or manage things at home?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that’s the part that’s started to really frighten me, honestly, because it’s not just that I feel bad privately and then I can still function normally. It’s showing up in very concrete ways.\n\nAt school, I’m falling behind on grading in a way that I don’t usually. I’ve always been the kind of teacher who stays on top of marks and feedback — maybe not perfectly, but reliably. Lately I keep putting it off, or I sit down to do it and I just stare at the stack or the online submissions and it feels impossible to start. Then it piles up, and the students are waiting longer for feedback, and I know that affects the classroom because I’m not as prepared for where they actually are.\n\nLesson planning has been the same. I’m still getting through the day, but it feels much more like scrambling. I used to have a sense of where a unit was going, what I wanted them to get out of it, what needed adjusting. Now I’m doing more last-minute planning than I’m comfortable admitting. I can get through a lesson, but it doesn’t feel like I’m teaching the way I normally would.\n\nThere was also a grading mistake recently that really scared me. Not because it was catastrophic, exactly, but because I used to be so meticulous about that kind of thing. I’d double-check, I’d notice discrepancies, I’d catch myself. And this time I missed something I really should have caught, and when I realized it, I just felt this awful sinking feeling, like… this isn’t me, or at least it didn’t used to be.\n\nAt home, I’m managing the absolute basics, but not much beyond that. And socially, I’ve really stopped showing up for people the way I used to. I don’t call family back as quickly, I avoid making plans with friends, I cancel things or let messages sit because even replying feels like another task. So my life has gotten very small. It’s basically get through school somehow, come home exhausted, do the minimum, and then start over.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and a half, was there ever a stretch of at least two weeks when you felt depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. It hasn’t really been just a short patch, unfortunately.\n\nI’d say this has been going on for about… a year and seven months now, roughly. It started around the time I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. But it didn’t really lift.\n\nAnd within that whole period, yes, there have absolutely been stretches of more than two weeks where I felt depressed nearly every day. Honestly, it’s been more continuous than I want to admit. Not really… at least, not that I can point to clearly.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I’ve started having thoughts that really frightened me. Not like I have some plan, or that I want to do anything, but I keep catching myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. And then sometimes, especially on the drive home after work, my mind goes to this awful place where I wonder if my family, or even my students, would somehow be better off without me.\n\nI know that sounds terrible saying it out loud. I’m a teacher, I’m supposed to be responsible and steady, and I don’t want to act on those thoughts. That’s part of why I came in, because the fact that they’re showing up at all really scares me.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts come up, do they feel like unwanted thoughts that pop into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s exactly what scares me. They feel unwanted. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think that way, or like I’m making some kind of plan. It’s more like I’ll be driving home from work, and out of nowhere this thought comes in — like maybe it would be easier if I didn’t wake up, or maybe my family and my students would be better off without me — and then I feel horrified that I even thought it.\n\nI don’t want to act on it. I really don’t. But the fact that it pops into my mind at all makes me feel like something is very wrong, and then I get scared of myself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts show up, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to make sure you wouldn’t act on them?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so? Not in a repeated way, at least. I’m trying to think if there’s anything I do like that, because I don’t want to miss something important.\n\nWhen the thoughts happen, I usually just get really upset and sort of try to push them away, or I’ll sit there feeling guilty and shaken by it. But I don’t think I have a ritual or a specific thing I do over and over to prove I won’t act on them. It’s more like I feel scared that the thought happened at all, and then I keep turning it over in my head afterward, wondering what it says about me.\n\nI mean, I do sometimes reassure myself that I don’t want to do anything, or remind myself of my family and my students, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like a repeated checking thing exactly. It feels more like panic and shame after the thought shows up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days lately?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I know I haven’t been feeling like myself, obviously, or I wouldn’t be here. But “down or depressed most days” — I don’t know if I’ve been calling it that in my own head.\n\nSome days I just feel heavy, I guess, or kind of worn out emotionally. But then I’ll tell myself that everyone gets overwhelmed, especially with work and everything, so I don’t always know where the line is. I’ve been trying not to make too big a deal out of it, and then something happens like these thoughts showing up, and it makes me wonder if I’ve been brushing things off more than I should.\n\nSo… maybe? I’m sorry, that’s not a very straightforward answer. I can say I haven’t felt okay lately, but I’m not sure I know exactly how to label it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Actually, yes, probably more than I’ve wanted to admit.\n\nI used to have things I really looked forward to, not just big things, but normal parts of my week. Like softball used to be this thing where even if I was tired after school, once I got there and started warming up with everyone, I’d feel lighter. And book club too — I used to actually read the book and have opinions and enjoy the conversation. I have a little garden at home, and normally I’d be out there checking on things, fussing over the plants more than necessary. And the robotics club at school — that used to be one of the parts of teaching that made me feel really energized, seeing the students get excited and problem-solve.\n\nLately, though, it’s like I’m physically present sometimes, but not really in it. I go through the motions. I show up because I said I would, or because people expect me to, but I’m not getting the same feeling from it. And sometimes I just cancel. I’ll tell myself I’m too tired or that I have grading to do, but if I’m honest, it’s more that none of it sounds enjoyable anymore. Even the things that used to feel like “me” just feel like obligations now.\n\nDoctor: For the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly still not sure how to say that exactly. Nearly every day sounds so definite, and I keep second-guessing myself because I don’t know if what I’ve been feeling “counts” as depressed or hopeless in the way you mean.\n\nI can say I’ve felt not okay a lot of the time these past couple weeks — kind of low, heavy, like I’m dragging myself through things. But I don’t always wake up and think, “I’m depressed.” Sometimes I just think I’m tired, or stressed, or that I need to get through the day and stop overthinking it.\n\nHopeless… I don’t know. That word feels intense. I don’t know if I’d say hopeless nearly every day. There have been moments where everything feels pointless or like I can’t see myself getting back to normal, but then I’ll also tell myself I’m being dramatic or that it’ll pass.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not completely sure. I’ve felt down a lot lately, probably more days than not, but I don’t know if I can confidently say nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, there was a specific work thing, though it didn’t feel like a single dramatic event at the time. It was more like a major change in what I was carrying.\n\nIt started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my regular teaching load. That was about… a year and seven months ago now, I think. I remember thinking I could handle it because it was important, and I cared about doing it well, and I didn’t want to be the person who said no. But it became this constant extra layer on everything I was already doing.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just a busy term, or that once we got through the next deadline it would settle. But it never really did. It’s hard to separate what started when, because teaching is always busy, but when I look back, that’s when things started changing for me. That was the point where I stopped feeling like I could quite catch up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way continuously for the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It feels like a long time, honestly, long enough that it’s hard to remember what I felt like before, but when I actually count it out, it’s been about a year and seven months.\n\nIt started around when I took over that STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my regular teaching. That’s the point I keep coming back to when I try to trace it. I don’t think I was feeling like this continuously before then, at least not in the same way. Since then, though, it’s felt pretty ongoing.\n\nDoctor: Have there been separate periods in your life before this past year and a half when you felt depressed and lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I don’t think there was a clear separate period like that before this, not one that stands out to me as “yes, that was definitely two weeks where I was depressed and lost interest in everything.”\n\nI’ve had stressful patches before, definitely — I mean, I think most people have — and times where I felt low or overwhelmed, especially with work or life stuff. But I don’t know that I’d describe them as the same as what’s been happening this past year and a half. I don’t remember a distinct episode where I stopped enjoying things for at least two weeks in a way that felt obvious at the time.\n\nIt’s possible I’m forgetting or minimizing it, because I tend to push through and not label things until much later. But if you’re asking whether there was a clear earlier period like this, I’d say… not that I can really identify.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. I mean, I do worry, but I don’t know where the line is between normal worrying and excessive worrying, especially when things feel like they actually need worrying about.\n\nA lot of it tends to be… practical, I suppose? Like whether I’ve missed something, whether I’ve let something slip, whether I’m keeping up properly. But I don’t know that I sit there worrying about every area of my life all the time, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like certain things get stuck in my head and I go over them more than I probably need to.\n\nSo… I guess yes, I worry, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as excessive worry about lots of different things. I’d maybe need you to clarify what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: If that work burden were removed, do you think you’d still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve thought about that a lot, actually, because part of me wants to say yes, of course, if the workload disappeared then I’d feel better. It would be such a relief not to have that constant pressure sitting there.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s that simple. I can’t quite tell anymore what’s caused by the work burden and what’s just… become the way I feel. I haven’t had a real chance to test it, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I suppose my honest answer is: I’d expect some relief, but I’m not confident I’d suddenly feel like myself again.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts come up, do they feel senseless or out of character for you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel senseless, exactly. That’s part of what scares me, I think. In the moment they can feel… almost logical, like my brain is making an argument that everyone would be less burdened if I just wasn’t there. Especially on the drive home, when I’m already exhausted and going over everything I’ve done wrong or might have failed at, I’ll catch myself wondering whether my family or even my students would be better off without me.\n\nBut then another part of me is horrified by it, because it doesn’t feel like who I want to be. I don’t want to act on those thoughts. I’m here because I’ve started having thoughts like it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up, and that really frightened me. It feels out of character in the sense that I don’t want to die, not really — I just sometimes want everything to stop, and then I feel ashamed and scared that my mind even goes there.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you lost interest or pleasure in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. So yes, I’d say I’ve lost interest and pleasure in most of the things I used to enjoy, and it’s been happening pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’m tired all the time, but then when I actually get into bed, my mind just… won’t shut off.\n\nI’ll be physically exhausted, like my body feels heavy and I’m desperate to sleep, but I’ll lie there replaying things from school — assessment deadlines, whether I’ve marked enough, whether I missed something important, some tiny mistake I made in a classroom conversation that probably no one else even noticed. I’ll think, okay, stop thinking about it, you need to sleep, and then I’m still lying there half an hour later going over the same thing again.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up really early, around 4 a.m. a lot of mornings. Not just briefly waking up and rolling over — I mean properly awake, with that awful feeling like the day has already started in my head. I try to get back to sleep, but usually I just sort of lie there, checking the time, calculating how little sleep I’ve had, and then worrying about how I’m going to manage the day. So yes, sleep has been really difficult lately.\n\nDoctor: If school were out of session and you had no work responsibilities for a week, do you think you would still feel this loss of interest and emotional heaviness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, school definitely makes it worse, because there’s always something to worry about or prepare for, but it doesn’t feel like this is only work.\n\nIf I had a week off, I don’t think I’d suddenly be excited about things again. Softball would still feel like something I should go to rather than something I want to do. Book club would probably still feel like another social obligation, even if I had technically “rested.” And my garden… I know that sounds small, but that used to be something I’d naturally drift toward when I had free time. Now I look at it and it’s like I can see what needs doing, but I don’t feel that little pull to do it.\n\nEven robotics club — which is work-adjacent, I suppose, but it used to feel different from the rest of school — it used to give me energy. I’ve always been the kind of teacher who stays on top of marks and feedback — maybe not perfectly, but reliably. Lately I keep putting it off, or I sit down to do it and I just stare at the stack or the online submissions and it feels impossible to start. Then it piles up, and the students are waiting longer for feedback, and I know that affects the classroom because I’m not as prepared for where they actually are.\n\nLesson planning has been the same. And this time I missed something I really should have caught, and when I realized it, I just felt this awful sinking feeling, like… this isn’t me, or at least it didn’t used to be.\n\nAt home, I’m managing the absolute basics, but not much beyond that. And socially, I’ve really stopped showing up for people the way I used to. I don’t call family back as quickly, I avoid making plans with friends, I cancel things or let messages sit because even replying feels like another task. So my life has gotten very small. It’s basically get through school somehow, come home exhausted, do the minimum, and then start over.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and a half, was there ever a stretch of at least two weeks when you felt depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Not really in a way that felt like a proper stretch, no.

I mean, this has been going on for about a year and seven months now, since I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load, and during that time I don’t think I’ve had several days where I thought, “Oh, there I am, I feel like myself again.” There might be a day here or there where I’m functioning a bit better or I’m distracted enough that it feels less heavy, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling back to normal for a few days in a row.

There have been moments where everything feels pointless or like I can’t see myself getting back to normal, but then I’ll also tell myself I’m being dramatic or that it’ll pass.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not completely sure. I’ve felt down a lot lately, probably more days than not, but I don’t know if I can confidently say nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, there was a specific work thing, though it didn’t feel like a single dramatic event at the time. But it never really did. It’s hard to separate what started when, because teaching is always busy, but when I look back, that’s when things started changing for me. That was the point where I stopped feeling like I could quite catch up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way continuously for the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It feels like a long time, honestly, long enough that it’s hard to remember what I felt like before, but when I actually count it out, it’s been about a year and seven months.\n\nIt started around when I took over that STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my regular teaching. That’s the point I keep coming back to when I try to trace it. I don’t think I was feeling like this continuously before then, at least not in the same way. It’s more like certain things get stuck in my head and I go over them more than I probably need to.\n\nSo… I guess yes, I worry, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as excessive worry about lots of different things. I’d maybe need you to clarify what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: If that work burden were removed, do you think you’d still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve thought about that a lot, actually, because part of me wants to say yes, of course, if the workload disappeared then I’d feel better. It would be such a relief not to have that constant pressure sitting there.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s that simple. I can’t quite tell anymore what’s caused by the work burden and what’s just… become the way I feel. If it were removed, maybe I’d improve a lot, or maybe I’d still feel flat and worn down because I’ve been in that state for so long. I haven’t had a real chance to test it, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I suppose my honest answer is: I’d expect some relief, but I’m not confident I’d suddenly feel like myself again.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts come up, do they feel senseless or out of character for you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel senseless, exactly. That’s part of what scares me, I think. In the moment they can feel… almost logical, like my brain is making an argument that everyone would be less burdened if I just wasn’t there. Especially on the drive home, when I’m already exhausted and going over everything I’ve done wrong or might have failed at, I’ll catch myself wondering whether my family or even my students would be better off without me.\n\nBut then another part of me is horrified by it, because it doesn’t feel like who I want to be. I don’t want to act on those thoughts. I’m here because I’ve started having thoughts like it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up, and that really frightened me. It feels out of character in the sense that I don’t want to die, not really — I just sometimes want everything to stop, and then I feel ashamed and scared that my mind even goes there.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you lost interest or pleasure in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Nearly every day, really.\n\nIt’s not that I never do anything anymore, exactly, but the feeling has gone out of it. I used to really look forward to things like softball, and book club, and working in my garden. Even the robotics club at school used to be something I genuinely enjoyed — I’d get excited about the students’ ideas and the little breakthroughs they’d have. Now I still sometimes show up, or I tell myself I should show up, but it feels like I’m just sort of… performing the actions of a person who enjoys those things.\n\nA lot of the time I cancel, honestly. I’ll think about book club and instead of feeling pleased to see people or talk about the book, it just feels like another thing I have to get through. With softball, I used to like the routine of it, being outside, being with the team, moving around. Now I’ll make excuses not to go, or if I do go, I’m just waiting for it to be over.\n\nAnd the garden is maybe the strangest one, because that used to be very calming for me. I used to notice little things — new growth, weeds, what needed pruning — and now I look at it and feel almost nothing. So yes, I’d say I’ve lost interest and pleasure in most of the things I used to enjoy, and it’s been happening pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’m tired all the time, but then when I actually get into bed, my mind just… won’t shut off.\n\nI’ll be physically exhausted, like my body feels heavy and I’m desperate to sleep, but I’ll lie there replaying things from school — assessment deadlines, whether I’ve marked enough, whether I missed something important, some tiny mistake I made in a classroom conversation that probably no one else even noticed. It just loops. I’ll think, okay, stop thinking about it, you need to sleep, and then I’m still lying there half an hour later going over the same thing again.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up really early, around 4 a.m. a lot of mornings. Not just briefly waking up and rolling over — I mean properly awake, with that awful feeling like the day has already started in my head. I try to get back to sleep, but usually I just sort of lie there, checking the time, calculating how little sleep I’ve had, and then worrying about how I’m going to manage the day. So yes, sleep has been really difficult lately.\n\nDoctor: If school were out of session and you had no work responsibilities for a week, do you think you would still feel this loss of interest and emotional heaviness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, school definitely makes it worse, because there’s always something to worry about or prepare for, but it doesn’t feel like this is only work.\n\nIf I had a week off, I don’t think I’d suddenly be excited about things again. Softball would still feel like something I should go to rather than something I want to do. Book club would probably still feel like another social obligation, even if I had technically “rested.” And my garden… I know that sounds small, but that used to be something I’d naturally drift toward when I had free time. Now I look at it and it’s like I can see what needs doing, but I don’t feel that little pull to do it.\n\nEven robotics club — which is work-adjacent, I suppose, but it used to feel different from the rest of school — it used to give me energy. Now I mostly just go through the motions, or I cancel things when I can, because none of it really feels enjoyable anymore. So yes, I think even without school for a week, that heaviness and lack of interest would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms started to interfere noticeably with your ability to do your job or manage things at home?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that’s the part that’s started to really frighten me, honestly, because it’s not just that I feel bad privately and then I can still function normally. It’s showing up in very concrete ways.\n\nAt school, I’m falling behind on grading in a way that I don’t usually. I’ve always been the kind of teacher who stays on top of marks and feedback — maybe not perfectly, but reliably. Lately I keep putting it off, or I sit down to do it and I just stare at the stack or the online submissions and it feels impossible to start. Then it piles up, and the students are waiting longer for feedback, and I know that affects the classroom because I’m not as prepared for where they actually are.\n\nLesson planning has been the same. I’m still getting through the day, but it feels much more like scrambling. I used to have a sense of where a unit was going, what I wanted them to get out of it, what needed adjusting. Now I’m doing more last-minute planning than I’m comfortable admitting. I can get through a lesson, but it doesn’t feel like I’m teaching the way I normally would.\n\nThere was also a grading mistake recently that really scared me. Not because it was catastrophic, exactly, but because I used to be so meticulous about that kind of thing. I’d double-check, I’d notice discrepancies, I’d catch myself. And this time I missed something I really should have caught, and when I realized it, I just felt this awful sinking feeling, like… this isn’t me, or at least it didn’t used to be.\n\nAt home, I’m managing the absolute basics, but not much beyond that. And socially, I’ve really stopped showing up for people the way I used to. I don’t call family back as quickly, I avoid making plans with friends, I cancel things or let messages sit because even replying feels like another task. So my life has gotten very small. It’s basically get through school somehow, come home exhausted, do the minimum, and then start over.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and a half, was there ever a stretch of at least two weeks when you felt depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. It hasn’t really been just a short patch, unfortunately.\n\nI’d say this has been going on for about… a year and seven months now, roughly. It started around the time I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load. At first I thought it was just stress from that, like, okay, I’ve taken on too much and I’m overwhelmed, and once I get through the next phase it’ll lift. But it didn’t really lift.\n\nAnd within that whole period, yes, there have absolutely been stretches of more than two weeks where I felt depressed nearly every day. Honestly, it’s been more continuous than I want to admit. Some days are a little less bad than others, but it’s not like I had a clear two-week break where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Around the time this started, did you go through a major loss or upsetting life event outside of work?\n\nPatient: Not really… at least, not that I can point to clearly.\n\nI’ve gone over that in my head a lot, because it would almost feel easier if there were one obvious thing I could name — like a death in the family, a breakup, something happening at home — but I don’t think there was anything like that around when it started. Nothing major outside of work that stands out as the beginning of it.\n\nThere were ordinary life stresses, I suppose, the kind everyone has, but not some big upsetting event that explains it neatly. That’s part of what has made it confusing for me. It felt like it began around the work pressure increasing, but outside of that, I can’t think of a major loss or life event that set it off.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and a half, have you ever had a period of at least a few days when you felt like your usual self again?\n\nPatient: Not really in a way that felt like a proper stretch, no.\n\nI mean, this has been going on for about a year and seven months now, since I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load, and during that time I don’t think I’ve had several days where I thought, “Oh, there I am, I feel like myself again.” There might be a day here or there where I’m functioning a bit better or I’m distracted enough that it feels less heavy, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling back to normal for a few days in a row.\n\nIt’s more like the same thing has just kept running in the background since all of that started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes, very much so. That’s actually been one of the more frustrating parts, because my job depends on being able to think clearly and make judgments.

Like, I’ll be marking student work and I’ll read the same response three or four times, and I still won’t know what score to give it. Not because the answer is especially complicated, necessarily — it’s more like my brain won’t hold onto what I just read, or I can’t compare it properly to the criteria. I’ll keep going back to the rubric and then back to the response, and I just sort of… stall.

And even small decisions take me ages now. Something simple like choosing which lab supplies to order can turn into this whole hour-long thing, where I’m going back and forth over options that I normally would’ve handled quickly. It makes me feel inefficient and honestly a bit embarrassed, because these are things I used to just do as part of the day.

But I don’t always wake up and think, “I’m depressed.” Sometimes I just think I’m tired, or stressed, or that I need to get through the day and stop overthinking it.\n\nHopeless… I don’t know. That word feels intense. I don’t know if I’d say hopeless nearly every day. There have been moments where everything feels pointless or like I can’t see myself getting back to normal, but then I’ll also tell myself I’m being dramatic or that it’ll pass.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not completely sure. I’ve felt down a lot lately, probably more days than not, but I don’t know if I can confidently say nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Did all of this start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, there was a specific work thing, though it didn’t feel like a single dramatic event at the time. But it never really did. It’s hard to separate what started when, because teaching is always busy, but when I look back, that’s when things started changing for me. That was the point where I stopped feeling like I could quite catch up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way continuously for the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. I can’t quite tell anymore what’s caused by the work burden and what’s just… become the way I feel. Especially on the drive home, when I’m already exhausted and going over everything I’ve done wrong or might have failed at, I’ll catch myself wondering whether my family or even my students would be better off without me.\n\nBut then another part of me is horrified by it, because it doesn’t feel like who I want to be. I used to notice little things — new growth, weeds, what needed pruning — and now I look at it and feel almost nothing. So yes, I’d say I’ve lost interest and pleasure in most of the things I used to enjoy, and it’s been happening pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’m tired all the time, but then when I actually get into bed, my mind just… won’t shut off.\n\nI’ll be physically exhausted, like my body feels heavy and I’m desperate to sleep, but I’ll lie there replaying things from school — assessment deadlines, whether I’ve marked enough, whether I missed something important, some tiny mistake I made in a classroom conversation that probably no one else even noticed. It just loops. I’ll think, okay, stop thinking about it, you need to sleep, and then I’m still lying there half an hour later going over the same thing again.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up really early, around 4 a.m. a lot of mornings. Not just briefly waking up and rolling over — I mean properly awake, with that awful feeling like the day has already started in my head. I try to get back to sleep, but usually I just sort of lie there, checking the time, calculating how little sleep I’ve had, and then worrying about how I’m going to manage the day. Now I look at it and it’s like I can see what needs doing, but I don’t feel that little pull to do it.\n\nEven robotics club — which is work-adjacent, I suppose, but it used to feel different from the rest of school — it used to give me energy. Now I mostly just go through the motions, or I cancel things when I can, because none of it really feels enjoyable anymore. I’ve always been the kind of teacher who stays on top of marks and feedback — maybe not perfectly, but reliably. Lately I keep putting it off, or I sit down to do it and I just stare at the stack or the online submissions and it feels impossible to start. Then it piles up, and the students are waiting longer for feedback, and I know that affects the classroom because I’m not as prepared for where they actually are.\n\nLesson planning has been the same. I’m still getting through the day, but it feels much more like scrambling. I used to have a sense of where a unit was going, what I wanted them to get out of it, what needed adjusting. Now I’m doing more last-minute planning than I’m comfortable admitting. I can get through a lesson, but it doesn’t feel like I’m teaching the way I normally would.\n\nThere was also a grading mistake recently that really scared me. Not because it was catastrophic, exactly, but because I used to be so meticulous about that kind of thing. I’d double-check, I’d notice discrepancies, I’d catch myself. And this time I missed something I really should have caught, and when I realized it, I just felt this awful sinking feeling, like… this isn’t me, or at least it didn’t used to be.\n\nAt home, I’m managing the absolute basics, but not much beyond that. And socially, I’ve really stopped showing up for people the way I used to. I don’t call family back as quickly, I avoid making plans with friends, I cancel things or let messages sit because even replying feels like another task. So my life has gotten very small.
